# PREFACE.

Everything perishes, everything ends here below: God alone is eternal and immutable. Empires, kingdoms, great fortunes—everything has its <term>revolutions</term>. <note>In this 18th-century context, "revolutions" refers to the cyclical turning of the "wheel of fortune" or the natural rise and fall of states, rather than a specific political uprising.</note> Yet usually, these occur only imperceptibly. They are foreseen or preceded by <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "accidens"; here meaning unforeseen events or symptoms of decline</note> that announce them. There has never been any [revolution] so prompt and so sudden as the fall of the Order of the Tem- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, referring to the <term>Templiers</term> (Knights Templar), the powerful Catholic military order suppressed in 1312.</note>

<summary>The author opens the preface with a meditation on the fleeting nature of earthly power compared to the permanence of God, noting that while most empires fall slowly, the Knights Templar fell with unprecedented speed.</summary>
<keywords>transience, divine eternity, revolutions of fate, Knights Templar, sudden downfall, historical philosophy</keywords>

As images of the sovereign Master <note>original: "souverain Maître"; a reference to God, following the contemporary belief in the Divine Right of Kings</note>, they are sometimes like Him on earth: both creators and destroyers.

Such was the fate of the Order of the Knights of the Temple <term>Knights of the Temple</term> <note>More commonly known as the Knights Templar, this wealthy and powerful military order was suddenly suppressed by King Philip IV of France and Pope Clement V in the early 1300s.</note>, the history of whose abolition we now undertake to write.

Nothing in the world was so brilliant, at the beginning of the fourteenth cen- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, referring to the "fourteenth century" (quatorzieme siecle).</note>

These knights, so poor at the beginning, so moderate, and so submissive to sovereigns, found themselves as rich as those rulers and desired to walk as equals. The King of France, Louis VII, writing to the Grand Master Blanquefort <note>Bertrand de Blanchefort (c. 1109–1169) was the sixth Grand Master of the Knights Templar and is known for reforming the Order's statutes.</note>, addressed him as being there *by the grace of God* <note>original: "par la grace de Dieu"</note>, and his successor, Philip II <note>Philip II Augustus (1165–1223), who significantly increased the power of the French monarchy.</note>, left him a bequest of 40,000 <term>livres: the standard currency of medieval France, originally based on a pound of silver</term>, a sum that was then sufficient for the dowry of four daughters of France <note>The "daughters of France" (*filles de France*) was the official title for the legitimate daughters of the French sovereign; their dowries were matters of significant state expense.</note>. Thus, the Grand Masters held the rank of princes in all the courts.

<summary>This page illustrates the Templars' transition from poverty to immense power, noting how French kings treated the Grand Masters as sovereign equals and bestowed upon them wealth comparable to royal dowries.</summary>

From the year 1129, they had withdrawn from the jurisdiction of the Patriarch of Jerusalem <note>The Patriarch was the highest-ranking church official in the Holy Land; by removing themselves from his authority, the Templars became an independent entity within the Church.</note> and had submitted to that of the Pope, Gelasius II <note>original: "Gelaſe II". He and his successors lavished the greatest privileges upon the Order. Innocent III <note>Pope Innocent III (reigned 1198–1216) was one of the most powerful figures in medieval history, known for expanding the authority of the papacy over both spiritual and temporal affairs.</note> granted the Templars the <term>tithes: a religious tax, usually one-tenth of all produce or income, traditionally paid to support the local clergy</term> of all the towns and all the lands they possessed.

# HISTORY
## OF THE ABOLITION
### OF THE ORDER
### OF THE TEMPLARS.

---

#### BOOK ONE.

<margin>
**1304.**
I.
*State of the Templars.*
*Dupuis, Condemnation of the Templars.* <note>Referring to the work of Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar whose historical account of the trial was a primary source for 18th-century writers.</note>
*Vertot, History of the Knights of St. John.* <note>René-Aubert Vertot (1655–1735) was a popular historian of the military orders, including the Hospitallers and Templars.</note>
*Grutler, History of the Templars.* <note>A reference to Nicolai Gürtler (1654–1711), a Swiss philologist and theologian who wrote a Latin history of the Order.</note>
</margin>

THE ORDER of the Knights of the Temple had never been so flourishing as at the beginning of the fourteenth century: their valor, their conduct, and their wealth allowed them to stand on equal footing with kings and the greatest <term>capitaines: here referring to high-ranking military commanders and leaders of state</term>. They had rendered great services to Christendom in Palestine; and although in 1291, they had been driven out, along with the other Christian nations, from the kingdom of Jerusalem <note>The Fall of Acre in 1291 marked the end of the Crusader states in the Levant, forcing the military orders to relocate to Cyprus.</note>,

<summary>This opening page establishes the prestigious status of the Knights Templar at the start of the 14th century, noting that despite their expulsion from Jerusalem in 1291, they remained as powerful and wealthy as European monarchs.</summary>

The Pope did not lack supporters, among whom the Knights of the Temple <note>original: "chevaliers du Temple"; referring to the Knights Templar, a powerful Catholic military order.</note> distinguished themselves quite imprudently. In truth, as the Order fell under the jurisdiction of the Holy See <term>Saint Siege</term> <note>The Holy See is the central governing body of the Catholic Church, headed by the Pope.</note>, being under its protection and in its dependence, and constantly having favors to obtain from it, it was not natural that they should imitate the clergy of France <note>original: "clergé de France"; the French bishops and priests who often sided with the King against the Pope during jurisdictional disputes.</note>, who had declared themselves loudly for their King. Whereas the Order was spread throughout all the states of Europe, the Knights had to be more careful in managing the Sovereign Pontiff; but they should not have declared themselves so openly for him. Without observing this political caution, they not only appeared to act in his interests, they even provided him with money; and one of them, who was the Treasurer of the Savings <term>trésorier de l'épargne</term> <note>The Treasurer of the "épargne" was a high-ranking official responsible for managing the King's personal and central treasury.</note>, advanced a considerable sum to the Pope from his own coffers.

The people, upon whom the entire loss fell, complained loudly and refused to carry their money to the Mint <term>Monnoie</term>. The Templars were even more indignant and irritated by this. They possessed great sums of money, being the wealthiest private individuals in France; their avarice led them to regard this event as one of the greatest misfortunes that could befall them. They spoke insolently. The people gathered in mobs, and the Templars cast offensive words against the prince into the crowd. These words emboldened the populace, who rushed to arms and attempted to use violence to block the execution of the edict.

It is not said that the knights themselves were specifically involved in this riot; but it is certain that two men who had belonged to the order, and who still wore its habit <note>The "habit" refers to the distinctive white mantle with a red cross worn by the Templars.</note>, placed themselves at its head and encouraged the crowd to push the sedition <note>A "sedition" is a focused rebellion or incitement of discontent against the established authority of the state.</note> further, in order to force the king to revoke his edict.

The king's wrath did not end so easily; the danger in which he had found himself, and the insolent audacity of the people, determined him toward vengeance. He ordered the arrest of those believed to be the leaders of the sedition, and he commanded that they be put on trial. The names of the two Templars <note>Referencing the disgraced former members who supposedly incited the crowd.</note> had been given to the king; they were arrested, and although no others had been named, it was rumored that the knights of this order had been complicit in the revolt, and had even fomented it. The king blamed them for it, and being already dissatisfied that they had declared themselves against him in favor of Pope Boniface <note>Pope Boniface VIII, whose bitter struggle with Philip IV over the limits of papal versus royal power was a defining conflict of the era.</note>, and that they had aided this pontiff with their treasury, he conceived a mortal hatred against the entire order, and sought opportunities to mortify and humiliate it.

The governor reported that, because of this, he had stayed the execution and was awaiting his orders.

He was so widely loved and esteemed that they easily succeeded in securing his election. 

Molay was the younger son of one of the most prominent noble houses in the County of Burgundy <note>The County of Burgundy, or Franche-Comté, was at this time a territory within the Holy Roman Empire, though culturally and linguistically tied to France.</note>. He rose steadily through every rank of the hierarchy until he reached the office of grand prior. He was a nobleman of genuine merit: brave, highly intelligent, and characterized by a gentle and moderate temperament. His morals were considered pure, and his conduct remained beyond reproach. He had always moved within the French court with great distinction.

XII.
Meeting of the King and the Archbishop of Bordeaux.
*Fleury.* <note>Referring to Claude Fleury, a 17th-century priest and ecclesiastical historian</note>

The King received this news with joy. He knew the Archbishop <note>Bertrand de Got, the future Pope Clement V</note> to be one of the greatest minds of his kingdom, a prelate full of wit and insight, but at the same time the most ambitious and the greediest of all men. These two latter qualities led him to believe that he would easily succeed in winning him over and obtaining from him whatever he desired. He informed him that he requested him to travel without delay to an abbey near Saint-Jean-d'Angély <note>A town in the Charente-Maritime department of France</note>, where the King would be, and where he had to communicate a matter of great importance to him, and one which concerned him personally.

There was nothing to which the Archbishop would not submit to obtain the sovereign pontificate; he promised everything and bound himself by the most solemn oath of religion. This was done upon the body of *Jesus Christ* <note>original: "Jeſus-Chriſt"; this refers to a "corporal oath" taken while touching a consecrated host or the Gospels, considered the most binding possible vow</note>. He made this terrible oath without any scruple, even though he should have been quite pained to commit himself to a thing whose justice and possibility he did not know.

This fourth condition, which the King kept hidden from him, was the abolition of the Order of the Templars, for which he had such a great passion, believing them guilty of all the crimes imputed to them—

John II, Duke of Brittany, who had accompanied the King, was mortally wounded and died three days later. Several other people perished. He recounted to him as an established fact the corruption of this entire order; the abominations with which it was charged, and for which he told him he had the proofs. The Pope was strangely surprised by such a proposal. To abolish the most famous of the military orders, the one most useful to Christendom, which served as its bulwark <note>The Templars were essential to the defense of the Holy Land and Christian interests in the East.</note>; an order spread across all the states of Christian princes, and composed of the high nobility of all the kingdoms—this seemed impractical. He was frightened by it, and then recognized the rashness and indiscretion of an oath he had made without full knowledge, driven by a criminal ambition, even profaning the most august and fearsome of our mysteries <note>This refers to the secret pact Philip the Fair allegedly forced Clement V to sign to secure the papacy, often said to have been sworn upon the consecrated Host.</note>. The obscurity and uncertainty of these crimes, and this oath—void by right and similar, in some way, to the **oath of Herod** <note>A reference to the biblical story of King Herod, who made a rash promise that led to the execution of John the Baptist; here it signifies a promise that is morally wrong to keep</note>—led a pope full of enlightenment to believe that he was exempt from keeping it. The power of the pope did not yet have sufficiently solid foundations, and he had everything to fear from the resentment of this imperious king.

It was therefore fear and policy that prevented the pope from letting the king see the repugnance he felt at granting him this request. Several months passed without the king taking any steps: he wanted to allow the pope time to arrange matters and establish his authority. # History of the Abolition
<note original: "Histoire de l'abolition">This title refers to the historical account of the suppression of the Knights Templar.</note>

<margin>1306.</margin>
Prince Guy was born in 1285, and in 1292, the Dauphin and the Dauphine <note>Referring to his parents, Humbert I and Anne of Burgundy, who were the rulers of the Dauphiné region.</note>, who loved him dearly, while arranging the inheritance for their children, assigned to him as his portion the Barony of Montauban, along with 200 <term>livres</term> <note>The *livre* was the standard French currency of the time; "en fonds de terres" indicates this was recurring income generated by the land itself.</note> of income from land holdings, and 15,000 pounds in silver. This was a very considerable <term>apanage</term> <note>An *apanage* was a grant of land or money given to the younger children of a sovereign to provide for them, as they would not inherit the primary title.</note> for a younger son in a century when money was still so scarce. He could have, therefore, cut a great figure in the world and enjoyed its pleasures, being the son of a sovereign. But having devoted himself to piety since his childhood, he had no sooner reached the age of twelve than, renouncing these great estates, he entered the Order of the Knights of the Temple. There, he was received with distinction, and he distinguished himself so much that around the year 1304, he was provided with the Grand Priory of Normandy.

He shared with him another project he had conceived, and which he perhaps believed would be favorable to this restoration. This was to form a union of all the military orders <note>This refers to the long-discussed proposal to merge the Knights Templar and the Knights Hospitaller (the Order of St. John) into a single, more powerful organization.</note>, which, having thereby become more powerful, could have sustained themselves on their own and would have no need for foreign assistance. He gave the proposal to the Grand Master, asking him to examine it and to give him his opinion.

# History of the Abolition

---

<margin>1306.</margin>
...to the abolition proposed by the king <note>King Philip IV of France, also known as Philip the Fair, who was the primary driver behind the destruction of the Templars.</note>, and to which this pontiff had consented perhaps with too much ease, hoped to elude it through the union of all these orders, which could have purged the Order of the Templars of the crimes attributed to them; or else, whether he only made this proposal to amuse him, deceive him, and divert from his mind the mortal blow they intended to strike against the entire order.

Regardless, Molay examined this project with great attention, and replied to it with a memorandum <note>original: "mémoire"</note> that he delivered to the Pope, which contained the insurmountable reasons that made this union impossible: these were the difference in rules, the inequality of assets, the jealousy of the leaders, the opposition of sovereigns, and the opposing characters and temperaments of the nations, as some of these orders were composed of the subjects of a single prince, such as the orders of Germany and Spain <note>This likely refers to the Teutonic Knights in Germany and orders like Calatrava or Santiago in Spain.</note>. The Pope yielded to his opinion and appeared to renounce a project beset by so many difficulties.

# Concerning the Order of the Templars

---

<margin>
1306.
Rumors of the Templars' crimes spread.
*Dupuy* <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French jurist and scholar who published a collection of documents concerning the trial of the Templars in 1654.</note>.
</margin>

The Pope <note>Clement V, the first Pope of the "Avignon Papacy," who resided in France under the influence of the French crown.</note> and the King <note>Philip IV, also known as Philip the Fair.</note> carefully concealed their plan to abolish the Order of the Templars; but rumors of the crimes described in the denunciation of the two apostates <note>Referring to former members of the Order, likely Esquieu de Floyran and a companion, who provided the initial accusations of heresy and immorality to the French authorities.</note> had already leaked out. Too many people had knowledge of it for it to remain secret. It reached the Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay.</note> and the other knights while they were in Poitiers; they went to complain to the Pope about it, calling it a horrifying slander; they begged him to investigate it thoroughly in order to learn the truth, and submitted themselves to the harshest penalties if they were found guilty. The Pope answered nothing; his silence signified that he doubted these crimes and that he consented to the evidence they offered to provide of their innocence. Indeed, it is likely that this pontiff, still undecided about the abolition of this order, wished that the knights might be able to justify themselves.

<margin>
The Grand Master returns to Paris.
*Dupuy.*
</margin>

As for the disregard for legal forms, it is always dangerous.

### of the Order of the Templars.

<margin>
1306.
Result of the king’s secret council.
*The same authors.*
</margin>

It was decided in this council that none of the ordinary rules observed in criminal trials would be followed; that it was necessary, without giving them any warning, to have all the Templars in the kingdom arrested on the same day and at the same hour, to hold them separately in different prisons, and at the same time to seize all their property, to prevent their friends and supporters from using it in their favor. <note>This simultaneous arrest, executed on Friday, October 13, 1307, was a logistical feat that allowed the King to catch the Order completely off guard.</note>

1307.

This was a guaranteed way to terrify them all, and to deprive them of the protection and aid they might have expected from these kings and the other knights, their brothers. <note>Philip IV used a sophisticated diplomatic campaign to ensure that the Templars had no sanctuary or financial resources left anywhere in Europe.</note> For the execution of these orders, it required a

<margin>1307. All the Templars are arrested in the rest of the kingdom. *Dupuis.* <note>Pierre Dupuis (1582–1651) was a French scholar and royal librarian whose historical works on the Templars' trial are a primary source for this text.</note></margin>
Throughout the entire kingdom, the king’s governors and officers opened their sealed packets <note>These were secret, written instructions sent out in advance with orders not to be opened until the specified date to ensure the element of surprise.</note> on the night of the 12th to the 13th; there they found the orders against the Templars, and immediately took measures to execute them, calling upon all the forces they had at their disposal. From the morning of the 13th, the houses where the knights were staying were invested; they were all arrested and led to prison. They offered no resistance, having had no suspicion of their misfortune, and being separated from one another.

The precise number of those who were arrested is not known; it must have been very great, both due to the extent of the kingdom and because France was the state that furnished the most knights to the order. Twenty-eight are named in Normandy: namely, thirteen at Caen, ten at Pont-de-l'Arche, and five at Bayeux; three at Troyes; and twelve in Languedoc, including six at Carcassonne and as many at Beaucaire.

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 64, which listed members of the Order of the Templars. The text transitions from the list of names to a description of their imprisonment following the mass arrests in 1307.</meta>

# History of the Order of the Templars.
<sig>F</sig>

<margin>1307.</margin>
All these knights were held in various prisons in Paris and the surrounding area, especially at Melun. They were not all kept separate, as there were not enough prisons for one hundred and forty prisoners; but for each location, there were lords tasked with watching over them to ensure that none could escape. It is noted that Hugh of La Celle and William of Marsilly were tasked with this scarcely honorable post *(a)*. They had subordinates under them who were nonetheless also men of quality, as they are named in the records as knights: these were Philippe Coquerel, Girard Robert, William of Bretigny, Jean de Boisemont, Imbert of Saint-Jara, and Jean Pitard. Their salaries were fixed.

<margin>The Father Confessor, Inquisitor of the Faith. <note>The margin cites "Dupuis," referring to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who published primary documents regarding the Templar trial.</note></margin>
Above them all, and acting as inspector general, was the King's confessor, William of Paris, a Dominican and

---

*(a) Scarcely honorable! Any duty useful to the state, granted by the sovereign, is honorable. We do not wish to be blamed for this more than thoughtless expression, which our duty to accuracy compels us to include.* <note>The author or editor here is likely responding to an earlier source that looked down upon the role of a jailer, even for high-ranking knights.</note>

<summary>This page details the imprisonment of 140 Knights Templar in 1307, specifically the logistics of their confinement in Paris and Melun and the identity of the noblemen appointed as their keepers and overseers.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, 1307 arrests, imprisonment, Melun, William of Paris, Inquisitor, King Philip IV, jailers, noble guards</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of William of Paris, the King's confessor, whose introduction began at the bottom of page 65. It details his collaboration with King Philip the Fair and the minister Nogaret to manipulate the imprisoned Templars into giving desired testimonies.</meta>

# History of the Abolition
<header>History of the Abolition</header>

---

<margin>1307.</margin>
<term>inquisitor of the faith</term><note>An official of the Inquisition, tasked by the Church to investigate and prosecute heresy. In this context, William of Paris held this role while also serving as the King's personal confessor.</note>; he was a very learned man who possessed the king's full confidence, but who was also so blindly devoted to him that the prince's will was his law. Everything had been coordinated between them—that is, between the king and the Father Confessor, to whom one may add <term>Nogaret</term><note>Guillaume de Nogaret (c. 1260–1313), a powerful advisor and lawyer to Philip the Fair, known for his ruthless tactics against the Pope and the Templars.</note>, one of the principal ministers, who shared the prince's views no less than the others. <margin>Mezeray.</margin><note>The margin refers to François Eudes de Mézeray (1610–1683), a prominent French historian often cited as a source for these events.</note> The Father Confessor had traveled to Melun, where the majority of the prisoners were held. There, he devoted all his efforts to ensuring they were well guarded; he saw them often, spoke with them, and suggested the <term>depositions</term><note>Formal statements or testimonies given by the accused during a legal investigation.</note> they were expected to make. To all appearances, he managed their state of mind to make them understand the king's intentions and at what price they might obtain their freedom. In his capacity as inquisitor, he took advantage of their answers to provide testimony on occasion—a testimony that his official rank was bound to make more effective (*a*).

---

(*a*) This trait is the only one a true Frenchman might disapprove of in the conduct of <term>Philip the Fair</term><note>Philip IV of France (reigned 1285–1314), the monarch who dismantled the Order of the Templars to seize their wealth and consolidate power.</note>: but it is the crime of the age.

<summary>This page describes how William of Paris, acting as both Inquisitor and King’s Confessor, manipulated the imprisoned Templars at Melun to extract specific confessions. It highlights the collusion between the King, the Inquisitor, and the minister Nogaret.</summary>
<keywords>William of Paris, Philip the Fair, Guillaume de Nogaret, Melun, Inquisition, depositions, confessions, 1307, Mezeray</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of King Philip the Fair’s preparations for the trial of the Templars, specifically focusing on the manipulation of public opinion and the formalization of the charges following the psychological pressure applied to the prisoners described on the previous page.</meta>

# of the Order of the Templars.
<header>of the Order of the Templars.</header>
<sig>F ij</sig>

<margin>1307.
Arrangements for the interrogation.
*Dupuis*.<note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar and librarian to the King, wrote a famous history of the Templars' trial based on original documents.</note></margin>
All of these precautions were intended to set the stage for the interrogation that the King wished to impose upon the prisoners, and to ensure it conformed to the ideas of the Prince, who remained fully convinced of the Templars' crimes and the corruption of their Order. As the time for this legal formality drew near, he publicized all these alleged crimes and arranged to have a <term>petition</term><note>A formal written request. Here, Philip orchestrated a "grassroots" demand from the public to give his personal vendetta the appearance of popular justice.</note> presented to him by the Parisians in the name of the French people. In this document, expressing their loathing for the "abominations" of the Templars, they begged him to prosecute them vigorously. The public was told of the knights' "enormous crimes": their renunciation of Jesus Christ; their contempt for His cross, upon which they supposedly spat three times; the permission of <term>sodomy</term><note>In the medieval period, accusations of "unnatural" sexual acts were a standard tool for destroying the reputation of political or religious enemies.</note>, which allegedly limited their vow of chastity only to avoiding relations with women; the preparation for this crime through "infamous kisses"; and finally, idolatry, which supposedly led them to worship an idol with a golden head and to gird themselves with a small cord that had touched the idol, which they regarded as an amulet.

<vocab>Templars, interrogation, Parisians, Jesus Christ, sodomy, idolatry, Dupuis</vocab>

<summary>This page describes how King Philip the Fair orchestrated a public propaganda campaign, including a staged petition from the people of Paris, to justify the interrogation and prosecution of the Templars based on sensational charges of heresy and immorality.</summary>
<keywords>Philip the Fair, trial of the Templars, propaganda, heresy accusations, Baphomet, public petition, Pierre Dupuy</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the legal proceedings against the Templars following their arrest in October 1307. It details the appointment of the King’s confessor as the lead inquisitor and the expansion of the interrogations throughout France.</meta>

<header>History of the Abolition</header>

---

<margin>1307.</margin>
It was the Father Confessor who was tasked with conducting the interrogation of the one hundred and forty Templars arrested in Paris: he claimed to have the right to do so as <term>Inquisitor of the Faith</term><note>A high-ranking official responsible for identifying and suppressing heresy. Guillaume de Paris, who held this title, was also the King's personal confessor, creating a significant conflict of interest.</note> and delegate of the Pope. In this capacity, he sub-delegated commissioners to the various provinces of the kingdom, to conduct at the same time the interrogation of the knights who had been arrested there. In order to make the process more authentic, the King ordered all <term>bailiffs</term> and <term>seneschals</term><note>Royal administrative and judicial officers who represented the King's authority in the northern (bailiffs) and southern (seneschals) regions of France, respectively.</note> to be present, along with the lords of the province.

We do not possess all of these interrogations; but by those that remain to us, it is easy to judge the others. These are the interrogations of Paris, Caen, Pont-de-l'Arche, Cahors, and Carcassonne; the one in Paris began toward the end of October, and lasted almost the entire month of November.

<margin>
Interrogation of Paris.
*Dupuis.*
*Guetler.*<note>Referring to the historians Pierre Dupuy and Nicolaus Gürtler, whose works documented the legal proceedings against the Order.</note>
</margin>
This interrogation, which was to be carried out in Paris and the surrounding areas, was the most important, and the one upon which the fate of the Order would undoubtedly depend, since—

<summary>This page describes the organization of the first formal interrogations of the Templars in late 1307, led by the King's confessor in his role as Inquisitor. It highlights the geographical spread of the trials and the involvement of royal officials to lend the proceedings an air of legal legitimacy.</summary>
<keywords>Guillaume de Paris, Inquisitor of the Faith, legal proceedings, bailiffs, seneschals, 1307, Pierre Dupuy, Nicolaus Gürtler, French provinces</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the legal proceedings against the Knights Templar in late 1307. It specifically transitions from the planning of the Paris interrogations to the logistical details of how the high-ranking members of the Order were to be questioned and pressured into confession.</meta>

# of the Order of the Templars.

<margin>1307.</margin>
...that one should hear one hundred and forty witnesses, and confront them with the principal Templars, such as the <term>Grand Master</term><note>Jacques de Molay, the final Grand Master of the Knights Templar, who was eventually burned at the stake in 1314.</note>, the Grand Prior of France—who was also called the Grand Commander—the Grand Priors of Normandy and Aquitaine, and the other knights, all from the leading houses of France. The <term>Inquisitor of the Faith</term><note>Guillaume de Paris, the King's confessor, who led the proceedings.</note> first traveled, according to all appearances, to Melun, where the greatest number of prisoners were held, all of whom were informed that the King desired that they confess the crimes being attributed to the Order. He was accompanied by the lords whom this prince <note>King Philip IV, also known as Philip the Fair.</note> had appointed to assist and be present at the interrogation. He first read to them the articles <note>original: "articles" — referring to the list of 127 specific accusations brought against the Templars, ranging from heresy to sodomy.</note> upon which he was going to interrogate them, taking their oath that they would all tell the truth: he then interrogated them one after another.

<margin>

*Gürtler.*
*The Spirit of the Laws.*
*Vertot.*

A terrible spectacle was joined to this formality. ### History of the Abolition

<margin>1307.</margin>
...with which they were charged. Because almost all of them denied these accusations, they were subjected to it <note>Referring to "the question," the legal term for judicial torture used during this period to obtain evidence for a conviction.</note>, but in a manner so harsh and violent that the limbs of many were dislocated *(a)*, such that they let out terrible cries, and the surrounding areas echoed with clamor and lamentations. The most steadfast among them endured these torments for a long time and confessed nothing, so that they were carried back to their prisons completely broken: several died there, protesting their innocence and the purity of their faith. Those who were not as courageous, after having suffered for a long time, finally yielded to the pain and confessed a portion of the acts attributed to them. But those who had the least resolve did not wait to be tormented at all; they testified to whatever was demanded of them, especially because they were made to understand that they ought to do so—

---

*(a)* If these facts have not been exaggerated by the historians of the time, this represents an injustice and a cruelty that we do not intend to excuse.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the interrogations of the Knights Templar in 1307, specifically addressing the psychological and physical pressures used to secure confessions after the initial refusal of the knights to admit to the charges.</meta>

### Of the Order of the Templars.

<margin>1307.</margin>
...to please the king, as they were assured of impunity and later of their liberty. Yet it is not evident that they all testified against the truth; for it could be that the refusal to confess these crimes arose from the shame of such an admission and the fear of punishment.

Such was the form of this interrogation; such were the means of persuasion and exhortation used by a priest, a monk, an Inquisitor of the Faith, to extract the truth from the accused. Such a procedure was as unheard of as it was odious, and it rendered their depositions extremely suspect. Is there, indeed, any example of starting by putting the accused to "the question" <term>the question: the formal application of judicial torture to extract a confession</term> when there is no proof against them, and making them suffer a punishment that is only warranted by a proven crime, or at least one half-proven?

The question, even when administered legally, is always a doubtful method and often produces the false as well as the true; the violence of the torments—

<summary>This page critiques the unethical and legally unprecedented use of judicial torture ("the question") against the Templars before any evidence was established. The author argues that confessions obtained through such "odious" means, including promises of immunity, are highly suspect and unreliable.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, 1307, Inquisitor of the Faith, judicial torture, the question, legal procedure, Philip the Fair, forced confessions</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of judicial torture ("the question") used during the trials of the Knights Templar. The previous page ended by describing how the violence of torments can break even the strongest will.</meta>

# History of the Abolition

<margin>1307.</margin>
...minds triumphing over the weakness of men (*a*); thus in *Athens*, one was subjected to it only for the crime of high treason <term>high treason: originally 'lèse-majesté', a crime against the dignity of the state or the person of the sovereign</term>; and when it was a matter of discovering accomplices, the guilty were only subjected to it thirty days after their conviction.

In *Rome* there was no preparatory question <term>preparatory question: the use of torture during an investigation to force a confession from a suspect before they had been found guilty</term>; birth, dignity, and ranks in the military always guaranteed protection from it, except in the case of a crime against the state; and even today in *England*, where the throne of liberty resides, this torture is not in use.

->Here is<-

***

(*a*) This is a completely useless punishment, since it is secret; for justice, always dispassionate, punishes only to set an example: it is dangerous, since it can turn against innocence. Those who think it is a standard part of the punishment have no understanding of our laws. Justice, we repeat, does not take revenge on the guilty: he is caught, he is dead <note>The author suggests that once a criminal is condemned, they are legally "dead" to society; the execution is then a tool for public instruction rather than personal vengeance</note>; she only uses his corpse to frighten the wicked.

<summary>The author compares the use of judicial torture to the legal standards of Athens, Rome, and England, arguing that it is an ineffective and dangerous practice that threatens the innocent. He asserts that justice should aim to provide a public example rather than extract private vengeance through secret pain.</summary>

### of the Order of the Templars.

<margin>1307.</margin>
Here, then, is what the one hundred and forty knights of the Temple testified, after that dreadful preliminary <note>The "preliminary" refers to the "preparatory question" or judicial torture described on the previous page</note>—both those who endured it and those who avoided it through a voluntary confession, in order to escape the torments they had seen some of their brothers suffer.

<margin>The renunciation of Jesus Christ and the cross. *Dupuis* <note>original: "Dupuis"; this likely refers to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who published a famous history of the Templar trial</note>.</margin>
Of the one hundred and forty Templars interrogated, there were one hundred and twenty-six who confessed that, at the moment of their reception into the order, they had been made to renounce *Jesus Christ* and spit three times upon the cross; they admitted to having done so. These are the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, 5th, up to the 23rd inclusive: 27th up to the 57th: 59th up to the 63rd, 67th: 69th up to the 77th: 79th up to the 97th: 99th up to the 114th: and finally, the 116th up to the 140th.

It should be noted that of these one hundred and twenty-six knights, there were sixteen who declared they had performed this renunciation and spat upon the cross only by force and the violence inflicted upon them. These are the 1st, 2, 7, 17, 52, 53, 63, 64, 68, 77, 81, 89, 103, 112, 126, and 139.

<margin>1307.</margin>
The 64th, Pierre de Villars, declared that he only renounced after he had been locked in a prison for a day and a night.

The 68th, Matthieu du Quenoy, said that he had been kept for three days on bread and water <note>A common form of "light" torture or disciplinary coercion intended to weaken the prisoner's resolve</note>.

The 77th, Constantin de Biciac, testified that to make him consent, he had been violently dragged through the entire apartment <note>original: "l'appartement"; likely referring to the suite of rooms or chambers where the judicial interrogation was held</note>.

The 81st, Jean de Buvine, stated that he suffered eight days of imprisonment.

The 112th, Elie de Jotro, testified that upon his refusal, he was beaten and imprisoned; but that they finally yielded to these violences, renounced *Jesus Christ*, and spat three times upon the cross.

According to the calculation, there were fourteen of these knights who did not speak of this renunciation at all: their silence is all the more surprising given that the superiors forced those who refused, and that within an order, the rules must be followed by all without distinction <note>The author is highlighting a logical flaw in the prosecution's case: if renouncing Christ were a mandatory rule of the Templars, it should have been mentioned by every knight, yet fourteen remained silent on the matter despite the pressure to confess</note>.

<margin>The small cord.
*Dupuis* <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French scholar and royal librarian who published primary documents related to the Templar trial, often used as a key source for this history.</note>.</margin>
There is much diversity in these depositions. Some never saw the idol; others did not worship it. Several add that at their reception, a small cord <term>cordelette: a ritual girdle or string that the Templars were accused of wearing around their waists after it had been "consecrated" by touching the head of their idol.</term> which had touched the idol was passed around their bodies, and that they were ordered to wear it always. Seeing himself under too much pressure, he said the word "I deny" <note>original Latin: *nego*</note>, applying it to the superior; after which he consulted a lawyer named Boniface Lombard, who advised him to make a formal protest before the official <term>Official: The presiding judge of an ecclesiastical (church) court, acting on behalf of a bishop.</term> of Paris, through which he would declare that the order did not please him.

### of the Order of the Templars.

Guillaume de Hautmenil.
*Dupuy.* <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French historian and royal librarian who compiled many of the primary documents concerning the trial of the Templars.</note>
</margin>
The 86th witness, Guillaume de Hautmenil, after admitting to most of the crimes attributed to the order, adds that he would have left it if he had not feared the reproaches of his family, who had gone to such great expense to have him received as a knight, and if he had not feared even more that such a move would be attributed to a lack of courage. He says that he had confessed these crimes to the Bishop of Poitiers; however, he does not explain what this prelate had commanded him to do—a man who, in all likelihood, should not have allowed him to remain in an order where he was forced to continue living in crime.

*Dupuis.* <note>Refers to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French historian who published a famous collection of documents regarding the trial of the Templars.</note></margin>

No trace of these frivolous imputations is found in the actual records of the interrogation.

They were further reproached for having stolen the royal treasure, and for having been in secret correspondence with the <term>Saracens</term> <note>A term used by medieval Europeans to refer to Muslims, particularly during the Crusades.</note> against Saint Louis <note>King Louis IX of France, who led two Crusades and was a model of Christian kingship; accusing the Templars of betraying him was a particularly damaging charge.</note>.

<margin>Interrogation of Caen. *Dupuy*. <note>Referring to the historian Pierre Dupuy.</note></margin>
It was the Dominican friars <note>Members of the Order of Preachers, a religious order often tasked with inquisitorial duties due to their theological expertise.</note> to whom the inquisitor had sent his powers who conducted the interrogation in Caen, in the presence of Hugues du Châtel and Enguerrand de Villiers, knights whom the King had appointed to attend.

There were thirteen knights of the Temple who had been arrested in this city, and who were brought out from the prisons to be interrogated by these Dominican friars, who promised the accused the mercy of the Church, at the same time that the King’s deputies assured them of the remission of <term>temporal penalties</term> <note>Punishments handed down by civil or worldly law, such as fines, imprisonment, or execution, as opposed to spiritual penance.</note>.

<margin>1307.</margin>
The instruments of torture <note>original: "appareils de la question". In legal proceedings of this era, the "question" was the formal application of physical pain to extract confessions.</note>, which were set before their eyes, compelled the first twelve men who were interrogated to confess to everything, except for idolatry. Regarding that charge, they declared they had no knowledge of it, asserting they had never seen the idol. They did admit, however, that each had been given a small cord <note>original: "cordelette". It was alleged that the Templars wore these cords around their waists as a symbol of their secret devotion to an idol.</note>, though the use they were meant to make of it was never explained to them.

The 13th knight refused to confess anything. He was immediately put to the torture; and in the midst of his sufferings, the promise of pardon and full mercy was renewed. Then, likely unable to endure the severity of the punishment, he followed the example of his brothers and made the same confessions.

We possess no further details regarding this interrogation.

This discourse having not at all deterred Cassagne, he was made to enter a hall where the superior was, accompanied by ten knights.

# of the Order of the Templars.
I

<margin>
1307.
</margin>
[The supe]rior showed him a bearded idol, which had the appearance of a puppet, and a crucifix; he made him worship the idol and spit three times upon the crucifix, telling him that this was the custom of the order. Afterward, Monpezat performed all the infamous kisses <note>The "infamous kisses" (*baisers infames*) refer to allegations by the Inquisition that initiates were forced to kiss their superior on the mouth, the navel, and the base of the spine as a sign of total submission.</note> upon the superior, who permitted him to engage in sodomy with his brothers to avoid relations with women—which would be ruinous to their reputation—and to better resist the extraordinary heat of Palestine. This superior also gave him a belt <note>Templars wore a small cord of white wool over their shirts as a symbol of chastity; however, during the trials, it was alleged these cords had been "consecrated" by being wrapped around the head of an idol.</note> which he drew from a box, and ordered him to always wear it upon his person.

The four other knights—Raimond Rubbé, Guillaume Bos, Arnaud Sabatier, and Pierre de Mossie—also testified to the same facts, with only a few differences in the circumstances. There was only one among them who added that the superior, while showing the idol, had uttered these Arabic words: *Sarrazin y Alla* <note>original: "Sarrazin y Alla". This is likely a corruption of the Arabic "Sahara il Allah" or "Ashhadu alla ilaha illa Allah." In the context of the trial, inquisitors interpreted this as evidence of Islamic influence or "Saracen" heresy.</note>, which mean *the Saracen to God*.

<margin>
Interrogation of Beaucaire.

In the context of the trials, this term was used by the Inquisition to claim that Templars viewed Christ as a false prophet or someone who had misled mankind.</note>. They further confessed that they had once worshipped the idol in a provincial chapter <note>A "chapter" was a formal assembly of the members of a religious or military order to manage their affairs or discipline.</note>, and one of them stated that this was the custom of the order.

The priest declared that the superior who received him forbade him from consecrating the host <note>The "host" is the bread used in the Eucharist; in Catholic belief, it becomes the body of Christ through consecration.</note> while saying the Holy Mass, and that he had forced him to swear an oath to this effect: which he had not always observed, instead consecrating it in his heart and whispering the sacramental words. The knights who took communion in the churches of the order therefore received only unconsecrated hosts. Some of the knights questioned at Beaucaire agreed to this.

# History of the Abolition

<margin>1307.</margin>

It is therefore not surprising that an order already disparaged for its luxury and pride was held in horror by the king, once he believed them convicted of these ghastly crimes, and that he was determined to abolish it. Nor is it surprising that once these crimes were publicized, all of France shared the king's sentiment, and that the Templars became the object of public abomination.

But by examining the circumstances of these interrogations more deeply, one cannot help but doubt the truth and sincerity of most of the depositions.

If it were a statute of the order, how is it that the inductions <note>Inductions: The ceremonial "receptions" (réceptions) where a new member is formally admitted into the Order.</note> were not uniform? Is there any example where, in an order or a religious community, the same ceremonies are not observed for all those who enter? And are not the rules and statutes explained to all of them equally? It is therefore evident, according to these interrogations, that there was neither a general rule nor a statute that established all these crimes, that imposed them, or that was known to all the knights; and that all those who made these general confessions might have done so only to escape their torments.

# History of the Abolition

<margin>1307.</margin>
Charles of Anjou, the second of that name, was its king, and lived in the greatest union and most perfect understanding with King Philip the Fair, of whom he was a first cousin.

This kingdom of Sicily had been founded by Charles I of Anjou, brother of Saint Louis <note>Louis IX of France (reigned 1226–1270), the only French king to be canonized as a saint.</note>, who had been invested <note>A formal legal ceremony where a person is granted ownership or authority over land or a title.</note> with it by Pope Urban IV, after having conquered it from Manfred of Swabia. This conquest included the kingdom of Naples and the island of Sicily; Charles I added the counties of Provence, Forcalquier, and Piedmont, which he possessed by right of his wife Beatrice of Aragon, who was their heir.

Charles II was one of the greatest and best kings in Europe; and however much he desired to please the King of France, it is doubted whether he would have entered into his views regarding the Templars if he had not had grounds for complaint against them, which he could not ignore, and for which he wished to punish them.

Nothing was grander than this establishment, had the French—with whom the king had achieved it—maintained toward the conquered peoples the moderation prescribed by religion and politics. But unable to restrain themselves, they treated the Italians insolently and irritated them by the liberties they took with their wives. The husbands' resentment was the origin of the *Sicilian Vespers* <note>A famous bloody uprising in 1282 that began at the hour of Vespers (evening prayer) and led to the massacre of thousands of French residents in Sicily.</note>, where all the French were slaughtered; after which these peoples placed themselves under the dominion of the King of Aragon, who took possession of the Island of Sicily.

This was the source of a long and bloody war between Kings Charles I and II on one side, and the Kings of Aragon on the other: the former made every effort to reconquer the Island of Sicily. Frederick implored the aid of the Grand Master of the Temple, who was then the famous Roger <note>The text likely refers to Roger de Flor (c. 1267–1305). He was in Greece, where, as in other parts of Europe, the Order possessed great wealth. Frederick had a large sum of money counted out to him—a lure that the Templars did not resist. The Grand Master crossed over to the island with a great number of his knights and the soldiers they kept in their pay. Charles’s troops could not resist this valiant militia, which drove them from the island and secured Frederick upon the throne of Sicily. They began to call this kingdom **Trinacria** <note>From the Greek *Trinakria*, meaning "three-cornered," an ancient name for the island of Sicily.</note>, because Charles continued to be called "King of Sicily," even though he should only have been called "King of Naples," since neither he nor his posterity could ever recover the Island of Sicily.

Outraged by this failure, Charles became the mortal enemy of the Templars;

The Pope <note>Pope Clement V.</note> soon learned of everything that had been done in France. <margin>Letters that the Pope wrote to the King.</margin>
In the first rush of his anger, Clement V wrote a very sharp letter to the King: he pointed out to him the injustice of his conduct, which directly attacked the pontifical authority; he added that he had not witnessed such an example from the kings who were his predecessors, who had always maintained the most religious respect for the Holy See.

### of the Order of the Templars.

<margin>1307.</margin>

This opposition from the King related to the duties that the <term>commanderies</term> <note>Individual estates or houses belonging to the Order, managed by a "commander."</note> and the <term>fiefs</term> <note>Land held under the feudal system in exchange for service and loyalty.</note> owned by the Templars were obliged to provide, both to the King and to the lords of his kingdom, such as <term>fealty and homage</term> <note>The formal ceremony and oath where a vassal acknowledges his duty to a superior lord.</note>, services, rents, and manorial rights. Since there was nothing but what was most just in the King’s request, the Pope did not hesitate to grant him everything through a <term>bull</term> <note>original: "bulle"; a formal decree or public letter issued by the Pope.</note> that explained the previous one. There was more difficulty, however, in confirming the administrators appointed by the King, since it was in the Pope's interest to have the enjoyment <note>The legal right to use and benefit from the income or "fruits" of a property.</note> of these assets, upon which his own rights appeared better founded.

The Pope being then disposed to enter vigorously into the King's project for the abolition of the Order, he issued on December 29 a <term>bull</term> <note>original: "bulle"; a formal decree or public letter issued by the Pope, named for the lead seal (bulla) attached to it.</note> which enjoined all persons, of whatever condition they might be, to arrest the Templars wherever they might be found, and to hand them over to the <term>ordinaries</term> <note>original: "ordinaires"; ecclesiastical judges, typically bishops, who have ordinary jurisdiction over a specific territory or diocese.</note>, with a prohibition against giving them sanctuary.

At the same time, by a second bull, he ordered that the commissioners who would be appointed to conduct their trial should be assisted by two <term>canons</term> <note>Members of the clergy who serve a cathedral or collegiate church.</note> of the cathedral, two <term>Dominicans</term> <note>Friars of the Order of Preachers, often involved in the Inquisition.</note>, and two <term>Cordeliers</term> <note>original: "cordeliers"; the name used in France for the Franciscan friars, referring to the simple knotted cord they wore as a belt.</note>; adding that if, during the trial, any matter arose which did not relate to <term>heresy</term> <note>The crime of holding religious opinions contrary to the official teachings of the Church.</note>, the commissioners could still take cognizance of it by pontifical authority, following the <term>canons</term> <note>The body of laws and regulations made by ecclesiastical authority.</note>.

# Concerning the Order of the Templars

<margin>1307.</margin>
Work thus began on the trial of the Templars; and the Pope issued as many as seven <term>bulls</term> <note>formal decrees issued by the Pope</note> to resolve the difficulties that had arisen both on the part of the King and on the part of the bishops and those in charge of the knights' assets.

<margin>
1308.
Decision regarding the retractions <note>original: "variations"; refers to the knights changing their testimonies or withdrawing previous confessions.</note> of the Templars.
*Vertot.* <note>René-Aubert Vertot, an 18th-century historian who wrote extensively on the military religious orders.</note>
</margin>
The bishops thus began to investigate the case against the order. This was a new procedure, and no regard was to be given to all the interrogations that had been carried out in Paris and in the provinces, since the Pope's authority had not been involved in them, and it was by virtue of his latest bull that they were going to proceed. However, this was neither the intention nor to the advantage of the Templars' enemies; for the knights might retract everything they had confessed, and thereby overthrow the project of the King, who remained determined to see the abolition of the order.

There was no small amount of embarrassment concerning the revocation of depositions made by several knights, both in France and in Provence. An assembly was held in Paris on this matter, at which

<meta>This page continues the description of the legal proceedings in Paris during 1308. The previous page ended mid-sentence, explaining that an assembly was held in Paris regarding the Templars' retractions of their previous confessions.</meta>

# History of the Abolition

<margin>1308.</margin>
the legates presided, and where the inquisitor of the faith and the king’s ministers were present—the latter being as zealous for the king's wishes as the inquisitor, who was the king's own confessor. They examined these revocations of the Templars' depositions, which were the fruits of the shame and repentance brought about by their previous weakness and cowardice. It was decided that no regard would be paid to these retractions; that they would be viewed as punishable falsehoods; and that the original depositions would be deemed truthful. It was further decreed that the prisoners would be enjoined to stand by their original statements and acknowledge their sincerity, especially since they were confirmed by so many other testimonies that had not been revoked. Finally, it was decided that if they persisted in their retractions, they would be proceeded against as <term>relapsed</term> <note>original: "relaps"; in the context of the Inquisition, a relapsed heretic was someone who retracted a confession of heresy, which was legally treated as returning to the crime and was punishable by death.</note>. Thus, at the same time that their formal trial was beginning anew, the interrogations conducted before the trial were established as its very foundation.

<margin>

*Dupuis.* <note>Reference to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who published primary sources regarding the trial of the Templars.</note>
</margin>

This was a consequence of what had been decided at the council held in Paris regarding the <note>original: "variation"; here referring to the inconsistency or retraction of testimony</note> retractions of some Templars.

<margin>Parliament of Tours. *Dupuy. Turtelin. Fleury.* <note>References to historical sources: Pierre Dupuy, a 17th-century scholar; Turtelin; and Claude Fleury, an ecclesiastical historian.</note></margin>
To accelerate this great trial, the King summoned a parliament to Tours for the month of June, and ordered nearly all the princes, bishops, and all the <term>great lords</term> <note>original: "grands du royaume"; the high-ranking nobility and officials</note> of the kingdom to attend, commanding them to appear in person or by <term>proxies</term> <note>original: "procureurs"; legal representatives authorized to act on their behalf</note>. He declared that he would be there in person, and that the purpose of this assembly was the affair of the Templars. Nothing could have been more disadvantageous for them, since the King’s inclinations were well known, and as feared as he was, no one would be bold enough to oppose him. By the second <term>bull</term> <note>A formal proclamation or decree issued by the Pope, named after the "bulla" (lead seal) attached to it.</note>, which concerns only France, and in which he repeats nearly the same points, he appoints commissioners—most of them archbishops and bishops of France—to travel throughout all the provinces to gather information against the Templars, to seek them out, and to interrogate them. # History of the Abolition

<margin>1308.</margin>
proximity of this abolition gave rise to a project that the ministers of this prince <note>This "prince" refers to King Philip IV "the Fair" of France.</note> conceived, and which would have been very favorable to him. They had designed it to gain his favor and to provide a grand establishment for Lord Philip, his second son <note>Philip, Count of Poitiers, who would later reign as King Philip V "the Tall." This plan reflects the French crown's desire to keep the vast wealth of the military orders within the royal family.</note>. The plan was to unite all the military orders of Europe <note>These included the Knights Templar, the Hospitallers (Knights of St. John), and the Teutonic Knights, among others.</note>, and to compose only one from them, which would have been called the *Royal Order*. They wanted to establish the head of the order in Cyprus, and to declare as grand master the King of Cyprus, Hugh IV <note>Hugh IV of Lusignan. Historically, there is some chronological tension here as Hugh IV was a child in 1308 and did not ascend the throne until 1324; the "ministers" were likely planning for the future regency or succession.</note>, who was unmarried and not yet of age. In addition to all the properties of the military orders, the kingdom of Cyprus and King Hugh's rights to the crown of Jerusalem <note>The title "King of Jerusalem" was still claimed by the Lusignan dynasty of Cyprus even after the city was lost to the Mamluks in 1291.</note> were to be assigned to the new order. With all the forces and all the riches of these orders combined, it would not have been difficult to conquer this latter kingdom. It was not doubted that this plan would be accepted by this young king, who thereby would have become a powerful monarch. For this to happen, it was necessary for this prince to take vows <note>To lead a military-religious order, one had to take monastic vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience.</note>. # On the Order of the Templars

<margin>

Nothing could have more flattered Philip the Fair <note>King Philip IV of France, known as "the Fair" (le Bel) for his handsome appearance, ruled from 1285 to 1314.</note>, who would have seen himself as the father of two kings and would have advanced the glory of the nation significantly. However, as ambitious as this prince was, being wise and skillful, he did not believe this project was practical. He foresaw the obstacles he would encounter from all the military orders, from the sovereigns in whose states they held commanderies <note>A commandery was a small district or manor under the control of a member of a military order.</note>, and from the Pope himself, with whom he did not wish to clash. Furthermore, the Grand Master of the Temple <note>Jacques de Molay, the last Grand Master of the Knights Templar.</note> had already dissuaded the Pope from such a design with such solid reasons that the pontiff was entirely disillusioned with the idea. Thus, this proposal was regarded as nearly impossible. Nevertheless, the king reserved the right to sound out the sovereign pontiff <note>Pope Clement V.</note> on the matter.

The Pope had written to him, ordering that they all be arrested on the same day; however, the secret was not well kept. They all took up arms, and it was no longer possible to make them obey through legal procedures alone.

The commissioners appointed to receive the defense of the Order were different from the legates who interrogated the prisoners. They were the Archbishop of Narbonne, the Bishops of Bayeux, Mende, and Limoges, Matthew of Naples (a notary of the Church of Rome), and the Archdeacons of Trent and Montpellier, all of whom were chosen by the Pope <note>Pope Clement V, who was under significant pressure from King Philip IV of France to dissolve the Order.</note>.

The surprise of the Grand Master, a straightforward man of true character, cast great suspicion on the reality of the interrogation in Paris, and on everything that had been done at Chinon <note>The "Chinon Parchment" is a historical document where Pope Clement V supposedly absolved the Templar leaders, though the trial continued under the King's pressure.</note>. The Grand Master asked for permission to confer with the Knight of Plaisians <note>Guillaume de Plaisians (spelled "Placian" in the text), a chief minister of King Philip the Fair. Upon the advice of Plaisians, the Grand Master returned to the commissioners and asked them for a delay to deliberate; he was granted until Friday the 28th.

On the 27th, a proclamation was issued inviting the knights to come forward to defend the order. As they had not yet made their arrangements, none appeared.

# of the Order of the Templars.

He added that a general alms-giving <term>aumône</term> <note>The distribution of food or money to the poor, a standard charitable practice for religious orders.</note> was performed there every week, and spoke at length about the service that the knights rendered to Christendom by defending it against the non-believers <note>original: "infideles." In this context, it refers to the Muslim forces the Templars fought during the Crusades.</note> with a valor and an experience in the military arts that had secured them so many victories. He recalled the famous day of Mansurah <note>The Battle of al-Mansurah (1250) in Egypt during the Seventh Crusade, where the Templars suffered heavy losses.</note>, where the Count of Artois, brother of Saint Louis <note>King Louis IX of France.</note>, perished because he refused to follow the advice of the Grand Master of the Temple <note>Guillaume de Sonnac, who was killed in the subsequent phase of the battle.</note>. The Grand Master had commanded the vanguard there and died performing wonders of bravery and leadership worthy of immortality.

The Archbishop <note>Gilles Aycelin, the Archbishop of Narbonne, one of the papal commissioners presiding over the inquiry.</note> replied to him that all these actions were highly commendable; but that they were useless without faith, which is the foundation of the Catholic religion and which alone can make such deeds meritorious. Upon this, the Grand Master, who agreed with this principle, made his profession of faith, which was entirely in line with all the dogmas of the Catholic religion; he assured them that every knight was instructed in these beliefs and practiced them.

They praised his piety and promised to use their influence <note>original: "s'intéresser"</note> to see this favor granted to him.

Jacques de Molay had famously retracted his earlier confessions of heresy, claiming they were extracted under duress or misunderstood.</note>. His answers made it clear enough that he believed it had been fabricated <note>original: "supposée," implying a forged or falsely attributed statement</note>; but the contrary appeared later.

# History of the Abolition

**1309.**
Manifesto of the order.
*Dupuis.* <note>Likely a reference to the historian Pierre Dupuy, who published primary documents regarding the Templar trial in the 17th century.</note>

Boulogne then read a manifesto that had been drafted for the defense of the order; it contained roughly the same points as his speech. However, as there are some differences, it is appropriate to report it here. He recited it with a fire and an unction <term>unction: a manner of speaking that suggests deep religious earnestness or spiritual intensity</term> that indicated his grief as much as his conviction of the knights' innocence. This manifesto pointed out that a holy order was being attacked, whose foundation was charity and fraternal love: that in its establishment, its only objective had been to help Christians against the infidels <note>original: "infideles"; a term historically used by medieval Christians to refer to non-believers, specifically the Muslim forces in the Levant</note>, especially in the Holy Land, at the cost of the knights' own blood: that the order had placed itself under the protection of the Blessed Virgin, and that it had been confirmed by the sovereign pontiffs <term>sovereign pontiffs: the Popes</term> who, in consideration of its services, had granted it great privileges: that by the grace of *Jesus Christ*, and by the help of this divine patroness, mother of purity, this order had been preserved without blemish in the observation of the three vows <note>The monastic vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience.</note>

<summary>Peter of Boulogne reads a formal manifesto defending the Templars, emphasizing the Order's charitable foundations, its military sacrifices in the Holy Land, and its devotion to the Virgin Mary.</summary>

### of the Order of the Templars.

<margin>
**1309.**
</margin>

...of poverty, chastity, and obedience, to which they had added a fourth: to expose their lives and to lose them if necessary, while fighting against the Saracens <note>original: "Sarrazins"; a term used in the Middle Ages to refer to Muslim opponents during the Crusades</note>.

The document then proved the unworthiness of the accusers, the invalidity of the depositions extorted <term>extorted: obtained through force, threats, or abuse of authority</term> by violence or by seduction <note>In this legal context, "seduction" refers to being misled by false promises of mercy or bribery</note>, and the revocation of the confessions made by most of the witnesses—an example that would have been followed by all the knights, had they not been held back by the threats made to them of being burned alive. It was maintained therein that the King <note>King Philip IV "the Fair" of France</note> first, and then the Pope <note>Pope Clement V</note>, had been caught off guard and deceived. It requested that all the knights be reconciled to the Church and restored to liberty and the possession of their property, so they might present themselves before the Pope, to whom alone they were answerable, and that they might be legally interrogated and judged according to the proper forms in a full council <term>council: a formal assembly of high-ranking Church officials to decide on matters of faith or discipline</term>. Boulogne presented this manifesto to the commissioners, who received it graciously and promised to pass it along to the sovereign pontiff <term>sovereign pontiff: another title for the Pope</term>.

# History of the Abolition

<margin>
**1309.**
Speech of the Knight of Monroyal.
*Dupuis.*
</margin>
Then the Knight of Monroyal took the floor, and delivered a military speech in which he became quite impassioned, and said that it was shameful that anyone had dared to attribute so many infamous crimes to a religious order; that the knights had been forced by tortures to admit to them; that the truth would be revealed before the Pope, their only natural judge; that none among them could escape his jurisdiction; that the Grand Master <note>The leader of the Knights Templar, Jacques de Molay</note> himself could not grant an exemption from it; that it was necessary for him to submit to it like the others, and that he should go to give the Pope an account of his deposition <term>deposition: a formal statement or testimony given under oath</term>, if he had been cowardly enough to make a false one. He emphasized the holiness and the regularity <note>original: "régularité"; refers to the strict adherence to the "Rule" or religious laws governing the Order</note> of the order, maintained that all the priests there performed the consecration of the body and blood of *Jesus Christ*, following the rite of the Catholic Church, and recounted the example of the Knight of Sens, who, out of a sense of religious scruple, had left the order to join a more severe one, but had returned to it, recognizing that he could only find salvation in the first.

They victoriously prove the fury of their enemies, the unjust and hateful plan to destroy such a venerable order, and finally, they demonstrate the falsehood of all the depositions of the knights, whom force and fear seduced into testifying to foolish fantasies and circumstances that were not even asked of them, believing they could thereby secure their future and gain the favor of the prince <note>The "prince" refers to King Philip IV ("the Fair") of France, the driving force behind the trials, who sought to dissolve the Order to cancel his debts and seize their assets.</note>.*

In Italy, matters had not come to arms, as all the Templars there had been arrested; however, the "war" waged against them was no less dangerous. These charges were not found to be strong enough, as the council deliberated whether to subject the knights to the question <term>The Question: a legal term for judicial torture used to extract confessions</term>. The Inquisitor General pushed for this course of action, but the fathers of the council opposed it; and after long disputes, it was decided that the case would be judged legally, discharging <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the next page likely continues with "discharging those who were innocent."</note>

Eight days later, in the same place in the field of Saint-Antoine, the second execution took place, which was the most terrible spectacle ever seen in France. Fifteen or twenty pyres had been lit—not in full flame, but like so many beds of fire to burn the guilty parties slowly <note>original: "insensiblement" — here meaning gradually or imperceptibly to prolong the suffering</note>, being filled only with glowing coals. An incredible multitude of Parisians and people from the neighborhood—from towns and villages for several leagues around—having been informed of this execution, had gathered there. Fifty-four knights, from among those who had retracted their first <term>deposition: a formal testimony given under oath</term>, were brought in carts; they were made to climb down to be thrown into this brazier and burned there over a slow fire.

They died in this way, consumed by a <term>slow fire: a method of execution intended to prolong the victim's agony by keeping the flames at a distance</term>.

# History of the Abolition

[The crowd] admired so many people of <term>quality: referring to high social status or noble birth</term> <term>sacrificed: the original French "immolés" implies a ritualistic or sacrificial killing</term> so cruelly. One could not believe men of such rank and such merit to be guilty—men who, in the flower of their youth, perished voluntarily and preferred such a horrible death to the life and the conditions offered to them. If they were criminals, was it not the height of extravagance to die for such a cause, thereby multiplying their crimes and appearing before the <term>Sovereign Judge: a reference to God and the Last Judgment</term> to be punished by even more terrifying <term>torments: "supplices" usually refers to judicial torture or execution</term>? And if they did not believe in Him, that inflexible judge, what interest did they have in renouncing life?

The entire populace was thus troubled, struck, and violently moved. It was the spectators who shed tears, who deplored the fate of the dying. Almost everyone believed them innocent, despite the <term>public voice: the official accusations or common rumors of the time</term>, which attributed such detestable vices to them.

Others, who believed them convicted based on such a great number of <term>depositions: formal written testimonies given under oath during the trial</term>, treated this <term>constancy: the firmness of mind or courage shown by the knights</term> as o— <note>The text cuts off here; the word is likely "opiniâtreté" (obstinacy).</note>

With this goal in mind, as early as May 12, he had written a long letter to the **Pope** <note>Pope Clement V, the first of the Avignon popes, who was heavily influenced by the French King</note>, in which he pointed out to him that because the crimes of the **Templars** were so enormous and so legally proven, His Holiness and the **Council** <note>The Council of Vienne (1311–1312), convened to decide the fate of the Templars</note> could not avoid exterminating them, abolishing their order, and creating a **new military order** to which all their property and privileges would be assigned. He added, however, that these assets could instead be granted to an **ancient order** with the same purpose; and he promised to enforce the Council's decrees on this matter within his kingdom, while always reserving his own rights and those of the prelates and lords of **France**.

Although the **Pope** and the **King** <note>Philip IV "the Fair" of France.</note> pretended to take no part in the final destination of these assets, and claimed they did not intend to profit from them, they nonetheless remained attentive to how the furnishings and revenues were being used. Thus, as early as May 12, while the Pope was in **Avignon**, he issued a **bull** <note>A formal decree issued by the Pope, named after the lead seal or *bulla* attached to it.</note> which ordered the **Archbishop of Rouen** and the **bishops of Poitiers and Mende** <note>Spelled "Mande" in the original French.</note> to demand an accounting from all the administrators appointed to oversee the **Templars'** revenues. They were also to ensure that whatever net balance remained was transported out of the kingdom under the King's protection. This was a clear enough indication of Avignon, and it made it known that the **Pontiff**, with the King's agreement, intended to dispose of these revenues himself.
