### of the Order of the Templars.

<margin>1311.</margin>
...proceedings in Italy. A council was being held in **Ravenna**, presided over by the archbishop of that city, assisted by eight bishops, his **suffragans** <note>Bishops who are subordinate to an archbishop within a specific ecclesiastical province.</note>, and three inquisitors of the faith.

Twelve **Templars** had been arrested, brought there, and interrogated. Since they would not confess to any of the crimes of which they were accused, it was ordered—at the request of the inquisitors (prejudiced men who always seek to find the guilty)—that they be put to **the question** <term>the question: A historical euphemism for judicial torture used by courts to extract confessions.</term>. Five were indeed subjected to it and confessed. The seven others were questioned without being **tortured** <note>The original French uses "tourmenter," which specifically refers to the physical pain of the judicial "question."</note>. They replied with the greatest firmness that all the accusations made against them were nothing but falsehoods. They were confronted with the five who had confessed, but this did not cause them to change their testimony.

Once the matter was brought to deliberation at the council, it was decided that the seven **Templars** would be interrogated again, but without putting them to **the question**, and without using any violence to coerce them into...

# History of the Abolition

<margin>1311.</margin>
...their depositions. They continued to answer with great assurance, and it was such that the fathers of the council <note>The "fathers" refers to the high-ranking clergy, such as bishops and archbishops, participating in the ecclesiastical assembly.</note> judged that they should be believed, and on the contrary, the depositions of the first five—which had only been made under duress—should be rejected. Thus, the result of the council was that these twelve knights were innocent. The decision regarding their fate was therefore referred to the Pope, because it was easy to understand that, regardless of the crime or the innocence of the Templars, the resolution had already been taken to abolish the order.

<margin>Dupuis <note>A reference to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar and librarian to the King, who wrote extensively on the trial of the Templars.</note></margin>
This was not, however, the sentiment of the fathers of this council; they were of the opinion that one should punish those knights who were found guilty, acquit the innocent, and—above all—preserve the order, if the majority were exempt from the crimes imputed to it. This was the matter upon which the **general council** <term>general council: Also known as an ecumenical council, this is a formal meeting of the universal Church hierarchy to decide on matters of faith and discipline; in this case, referring to the upcoming Council of Vienne.</term> was to pronounce.

<margin>
In Castile and in Aragon.
*Dupuis.* <note original: "Dupuis">A reference to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), the French scholar whose work "Histoire de l'ordre militaire des Templiers" is a primary source for this account.</note>

In **Castile**, although the Council of **Salamanca** had declared the arrested knights innocent, they were not set at liberty, and the king had taken possession of all their property. They awaited the decision of the **general council** <term>general council: The Council of Vienne (1311–1312), called by Pope Clement V to decide the fate of the Templars.</term>.

In **Aragon**, Don Artaud de Luna, the Grand Justiciar <note>The "Grand-Justicier" was a high-ranking official responsible for the administration of justice and often led military campaigns for the crown.</note>, who had been named general of the royal army, vigorously pursued the Templars who had retreated to their strongholds, where they defended themselves valiantly. But what could they do against the forces of an entire kingdom, without help and hated by all the people? Thus, de Luna besieged them from place to place, successively taking **Monzón**, **Miravet**, **Cantavieja**, and **Castellote**. The siege of **Monzón**, the strongest of all these towns, was very long, but in the end, they had to yield. All the knights were captured and led to various prisons.

<margin>1311.</margin>

The Elector, terrified as he was, did not contest this alleged miracle—which was entirely false <note>The author provides a skeptical historical perspective here, dismissing the claim of divine intervention as a fabrication used for the Templars' defense.</note>; he only repeated that they could all remain calm, and that the Pope would certainly take the council's intercession into account.

The council opened on Friday, October 16; the pope spoke there with dignity, but with passion. He exposed all the crimes of which the Templars were accused, and of which he believed them convicted. He detailed the legal proceedings and everything that had been done against them; and, detesting an order he considered so odious and so scandalous, he proposed to abolish it.

All the fathers were consulted on this abolition. There were two opinions on the matter: the first was moderate and appeared to be the most just; it concluded that the accused should be heard in their own defense, that all legal formalities should be observed in an affair of such great importance, and that the knights should be confronted by their accusers. # History of the Abolition

<margin>1311.</margin>

They were the intended beneficiaries of the Templars' confiscated wealth.</note> should benefit from the spoils of the Templars. He held long discussions on this matter with the ambassadors; but those representing the King of Aragon <note>James II of Aragon, a key figure in the *Reconquista*, the centuries-long effort by Christian kingdoms to retake the Iberian Peninsula.</note> stood firm. They made it clearly understood on his behalf to the Pope that this prince would not obey his bull <note>A "bull" is an official papal decree, named for the *bulla* or lead seal attached to it.</note>, and that since he had to withstand all the efforts of the non-believers <note>original: *infideles*; here referring to Islamic forces.</note>, it was more just and more necessary that the ecclesiastical assets situated within his states be employed to preserve them, rather than to increase the power of the Knights of Rhodes, who were less useful to Christendom than his own subjects, whose danger was more pressing. The Pope, who was wise and saw all the other sovereigns of Spain in the same disposition, did not dare to contend with them; he saw, moreover, that their claims were well-founded. Thus, he relented and consented that the King of Aragon should found a new order of military knights <note>This would eventually become the Order of Montesa, established specifically to hold the former Templar lands in Aragon and Valencia.</note> in his kingdom, destined to fight the Moors <note>original: *Mores*; the Muslim inhabitants of the Iberian Peninsula.</note>, and that the seventeen towns and castles [should be applied there]...

# Concerning the Order of the Templars

<margin>1312.</margin>
it was decreed that all the Templars should be condemned, and that their order should be extinguished and abolished.

The bull <note>A formal proclamation or edict issued by the Pope, named after the lead seal (*bulla*) attached to it.</note> regarding this was published on May 6 by the Pope, with general consent. It stated that the sovereign pontiff, together with the holy council, having found the knights of the Order of the Temple of Jerusalem guilty of the greatest and most enormous crimes, had suppressed, extinguished, and abolished this order. This was done not in the form of a definitive sentence—as the legal proceedings carried out against them could not provide sufficient grounds to condemn these knights through a judicial verdict—but rather by way of provision <note>In canon law, 'by way of provision' or 'apostolic provision' refers to an administrative action taken by the Pope for the perceived good of the Church, bypassing the need for a final legal judgment of guilt.</note>, by ecclesiastical constitution, and according to the fullness of his power. He forbade any person from entering the order, from taking its now-abolished name, or from wearing its habit, under penalty of excommunication <note>A severe ecclesiastical punishment that excludes a person from the sacramental life of the Church.</note>, while reserving the disposal of the Templars' property to the Holy See <note>The central administration of the Catholic Church and the office of the Pope.</note>. As for the individual members, he confirmed the sentences of the provincial councils of Sens and Reims, which he reiterated

<term>Templars</term> — A Catholic military order founded in the 12th century to protect pilgrims to the Holy Land.
<term>Bull</term> — An official papal document.
<term>Pope</term> — The Bishop of Rome and head of the Catholic Church.
<term>Council</term> — A formal meeting of church leaders to decide on matters of doctrine and administration.
<term>Jerusalem</term> — The holy city where the Order was founded.
<term>Excommunication</term> — The act of cutting someone off from the church community and its sacraments.
<term>Sens</term> and <term>Reims</term> — Major French archbishoprics where local trials of Templars had already taken place.

<summary>This page describes the formal publication of the papal bull in May 1312 that officially abolished the Knights Templar. # History of the Abolition
<page-num>262</page-num>

<margin>1312.</margin>It was to this order <note>The Teutonic Knights, mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> that all the princes of Germany were inclined to join the property of the Templars, in order to enlarge it, enrich it, and provide grand estates <note>Original: "établissemens," referring to the practice of securing lucrative and prestigious positions for younger sons within the military orders.</note> for their children.

The papal bull <note>The bull *Ad providam* (1312), which officially transferred Templar properties to the Hospitallers.</note> was opposed to this. An entire council, along with the Pope, had pronounced in favor of the Knights of Rhodes <note>The Order of the Hospital of Saint John of Jerusalem, who had moved their headquarters to Rhodes in 1310.</note>, and the other states had complied, with the exception of the kings of Spain. However, their example was contagious, and the indulgence that had been shown to them emboldened the German princes to demand that at least a portion of the Templars' property be awarded to the Teutonic Order, since it had originated from the generosity of their ancestors.

Because this demand was made in a tone that made it clear they would not obey if it were not granted, the Pope relented once more and consented that these assets be divided between the Teutonic Knights and the Knights of Rhodes. This was carried out accordingly, and through the division that followed, the wealthy commandery of Moriendal <note>Likely Marienthal or Mergentheim, which became a central seat for the Teutonic Order.</note> fell to...

# Of the Order of the Templars.
<page-num>263</page-num>

<margin>1312.</margin>
...to the first <note>Referring to the Teutonic Knights mentioned on the previous page.</note>, besides several other smaller commanderies <note>A commandery was an estate or manor under the jurisdiction of a member of a military order.</note>. Moreover, no bloody executions took place in Germany.

<margin>In Italy. Dupuis. Gurtler.</margin> <note>Historical references to Pierre Dupuy and Nicolaus Gürtler, authors who wrote extensively on the Templars.</note>
In Italy, the papal bull of union <note>The decree *Ad providam*, which officially transferred Templar properties to the Knights of Rhodes.</note> with the Knights of Rhodes was carried out to the letter. They were put in possession of all the assets of the Templars, and they remain in possession of them to this day. An exception must be made, however, in the states of the King of Naples for all the movable property, which the prince confiscated for his own profit—doubtless to cover the expenses he had incurred within his kingdom, and especially in Provence <note>At this time, the House of Anjou ruled both the Kingdom of Naples and the County of Provence.</note>. The sentence condemning relapsed heretics to the fire was strictly executed.

The Knights of Rhodes, therefore, did not benefit from all of the Templars' wealth; their movable goods, which were of immense value, were applied to the costs incurred during their trial. As we have seen, in France the King took for himself two-thirds of the movable assets in his kingdom, and there are many authors who claim that the Pope, so greedy for riches, did not forget his own interests, and that there entered into his coffers...

<page-num>265</page-num>

<margin>

</margin>
...hard captivity. It is true that in their depositions <note>Legal testimonies given under oath, often extracted under duress during the medieval Inquisition.</note> they had confessed to a portion of the crimes attributed to the order; but after so many bloody executions, one could imagine that regard would be shown for their rank, their birth, and their long imprisonment, which is no small punishment. This was not the case, and the Pope, by a bull <note>A formal decree or public letter issued by the Pope, named after the "bulla" or lead seal used to authenticate it.</note>, appointed as commissioners to judge them in his name and as a final authority the cardinal-legates, the first of whom was Bernard d'Aux, Bishop of Albano. He associated with them the Archbishop of Sens and the bishops who were assembled in Paris, even enjoining them to seek the assistance of several doctors of Theology <note>High-level academic experts in religious law and scripture.</note>.

The four Templars to be judged, whom the Pope had reserved for himself until then, were the Grand Master Jacques de Molay, the Prince Dauphin Gui <note>Gui of Auvergne, also known as the Dauphin of Auvergne, who served as the Grand Prior of Normandy.</note>, Grand Prior of Normandy, Hugues de Peralde or Peyraud, Grand Prior of Aquitaine, and a commander named Benigne Cœur-de-Roi, who had served as the minister of finances.

The cardinal-legates traveled to Paris in March before Easter, and assembled with all the commissioners. Under the law of the time, relapsed heretics were handed over to the secular authorities to be burned.</note>. Thus, to resolve the indecision of the commissioners, to prevent their dangerous pity, and to finish the abolition of a loathsome order—remnants of which would seem to persist as long as two subjects of such high rank remained alive—he <note>King Philip IV, known as Philip the Fair.</note> assembled those of his council who were with him and deliberated with them, without even calling upon any clergyman; and it was resolved in this **private council**, where the King's will was law, that without losing a moment of time, the two guilty men would be dispatched, in accordance with what had been practiced for the other relapsed heretics. Immediately, the King ordered that a pyre be lit on the island, situated between the King's gardens and the **Augustinians** <note>The monastery of the *Grands Augustins*. ...the officers of justice took him and tied him to the stake. The Prince-Dauphin <note>This refers to Geoffroi de Charney, the Preceptor of Normandy. They were both burned over a slow fire <note>original: "à petit feu"; a gruesome execution method designed to prolong the victim's life and agony by using low heat and avoiding large flames that might cause quick suffocation.</note>; what reveals this is that there were no rising flames that could have suffocated them; consequently, they bore all the severity of this terrifying execution. They suffered it with admirable gentleness and steadfastness, without cries or groans: they implored the mercy of God, and even when half-burned, they continued to renounce their false testimony <term>deposition</term>, while attesting to the holiness and innocence of their Order. Both died with these sentiments, and in some manner with a kind of consolation *(a)*.

---

*(a)* Inconceivable labyrinth of the human heart! That men who, to avoid torture <term>question</term> <note>The "question" was the formal legal term for judicial torture used to extract confessions during this period.</note>, confessed to false crimes, would then suffer the most horrific of executions with such steadfastness! But we believe we can glimpse the reason for it. It is that even the most resolute man, who finds himself caught...

<meta>This page continues the account of the execution of Jacques de Molay and Geoffroi de Charney. # History of the Abolition
<header>of the Order of the Templars.</header>

<margin>
1313.
Turmoil, horror & grief of the people.
The same characters.
</margin>
It is easy to judge what turmoil, what horror, and what grief seized the people and all the spectators at the sight of this execution <term>supplice</term>. If, ordinarily, at the execution of even the greatest villains, one shows pity and compassion, what were the feelings of the French upon seeing two men of such great standing perish in such a cruel death! The Grand Master of a famous Order, one who stood as an equal to sovereigns;

---

& in the hands of a powerful enemy, he is at first cast down and placed below his own station. Gradually, he becomes accustomed to his condition and recovers his natural greatness of soul; what do I say? Greater still, he adds to it the powerful impulse of indignation. We are far from approving the barbaric conduct of Philip the Fair <note>Philip IV (1268–1314), the French King who orchestrated the trial of the Templars to seize their assets and dissolve their influence.</note>: it was necessary to dismantle the order, keep its property, reunite it with the royal domain, or better yet, lease it <term>à cens</term> <note>A feudal land-tenure system where land was granted in exchange for a fixed annual payment.</note> to industrious subjects, and grant the former Templars their freedom along with a pension. These alleged crimes did not have a shadow of plausibility. But such was the misfortune of the times, that such [crimes] were required to abolish a corporate body that was so closely tied to the clergy.

<vocab>Order of the Templars, Grand Master, Philip the Fair, execution, former Templars, clergy</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the profound shock and sympathy felt by the French public during the execution of the Templar leaders and offers a scathing critique of King Philip the Fair's methods. The author suggests that while the Order's dissolution may have been politically necessary, the use of false accusations and brutal execution was an act of unnecessary barbarism.</summary>

# History of the Abolition
<header>of the Order of the Templars.</header>

<margin>1313.</margin>
—a prince, the son of a sovereign, allied to all the kings, and the son of a princess of the blood! Did the people not accuse the king of cruelty, and of failing to respect his own blood? The Grand Master <term>grand-maître</term> <note>Jacques de Molay, the final leader of the Templars.</note> was still in the strength of his age, and the Prince-Dauphin <term>prince-dauphin</term> <note>Referring to Geoffroi de Charney, Preceptor of Normandy, whose family held the title of Dauphin of Auvergne.</note> was in the flower of his, being only thirty-five years old. They protested their innocence, and the words of the dying—when they have the choice not to die—are regarded as oracles. Thus, almost this entire assembly believed them innocent. The crowd surrendered to grief; they shed abundant tears. Disturbed, afflicted, and even admiring their steadfastness, they looked upon these two lords as two martyrs of the truth, and posterity has rendered them the same justice. After their death, the people gathered their ashes, and many carried home whatever they could collect of their bones, like precious relics <term>reliques</term> which they preserved dearly and even revered.

native air would allow him to recover his health. He boarded a litter to travel to Bordeaux, where he was born; but the movement of the litter worsened his ailment, and he was forced to stop at Roquemande <note>Modern-day Roquemaure.</note>, a small town on the Rhône, near Avignon, in the diocese of Nîmes, and he died there on April 20, 1314, precisely forty days after the death of the Grand Master <term>grand-maître</term> <note>The supreme head of the Knights Templar, Jacques de Molay.</note>.

This pontiff possessed much wit and talent; but he sacrificed everything to politics, ambition, and a thirst for gold. He was not even exempt from reproaches regarding his purity, and he was accused of an illegitimate passion for the Countess of Périgord, daughter of the Count of Foix, who was considered the most beautiful person in France. He left immense treasures to his nephews, which, having been amassed by the most wicked means, were dissipated in a short time.

### of the Order of the Templars.

<margin>1314.</margin>
[social] man <note>The text completes the word "L'homme" from the previous page.</note> must step outside the rules of nature, and make of life a sacrifice owed to the truth. They both met a very disastrous end: what leads one to judge that they were shown some indulgence is that they did not die enclosed within four walls according to the terms of their sentence, and yet they hardly repented—either of their crimes, if they were guilty, or of their cowardice, if they were innocent. <note>This refers to the surviving leaders who accepted their life sentences in silence, unlike Jacques de Molay and Geoffroi de Charney, who were burned at the stake for retracting their confessions.</note>

Thus ended an illustrious order, which had shone for two centuries, which had rendered such great services to Christendom through heroic valor and brilliant exploits; but which had acquired prodigious wealth; a fatal source of pride and corruption, which made it as odious to Christian princes as it was fearsome to the infidels <note>original: *infideles*; a historical term used by the author to refer to the Muslim opponents of the Crusades</note>. That crime and debauchery had been introduced among many of the Templars is indubitable: that the entire order was infected by them is against all likelihood, and even against the truth. It succumbed <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, likely continuing to explain the external pressures that destroyed the Order.</note>

# History of the Abolition

<margin>1314.</margin>
under the hatred of a proud, absolute, powerful, and vindictive prince <note>This refers to King Philip IV of France, known as Philip the Fair.</note>. One cannot doubt that he was persuaded that they were criminals, and that this persuasion may have allowed him to find grace before God; but he set appearances against himself by a violent, cruel, and unusual procedure, although it must be admitted that if he had followed the standard legal forms, he would never have succeeded in abolishing an order so powerful and so numerous. The pope <note>Pope Clement V, the first of the Avignon popes, who was heavily influenced by the French crown.</note> was even more guilty than he, by cowardly yielding to the King's passion, and by seeing himself reduced to condemning this great order by way of provision and authority <note>original: *par voie de provision et d'autorité*; this means the Pope suppressed the Order as an administrative act rather than a formal judicial sentence, because the crimes had not been legally proven.</note>, and not legally. One cannot thus deny that a base interest <note>Refers to greed or sordid motives regarding the Order's wealth.</note> played a large part in this unworthy maneuver *(a)*, and that the pope, the king, and their ministers profited from a portion of the spoils of these unfortunate men.

---

*(a) Unworthy, not as to the substance, but by the means employed: without any other motive than the public utility, a king and a pope can abolish any order whatsoever.*

<vocab>abolition, prince, pope, king, procedure, order, 1314, public utility</vocab>

# of the Order of the Templars.

Aa ij

<margin>1314.</margin>
The only benefit that religion gained from the abolition of the Templars was the merging of all their commanderies <note>A commandery was a manor or estate under the control of a commander in a military order.</note> into the Order of Saint John—known today as the Order of Malta—which inherited both their property and their courage, and which serves as the shield and the bulwark of Christendom. May this Order, taking heed of this terrible example <note>Refers to the violent destruction of the Templars as a warning against corruption or loss of purpose.</note>, persevere in virtue and make itself more commendable from day to day!

---

->THE END.<-

<summary>This concluding page notes that the Templars' assets and military role were transferred to the Order of Saint John (Order of Malta), ending with a hope that the newer Order learns from the Templars' fate.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded the historical narrative of the Knights Templar's dissolution in 1314, noting that their assets were transferred to the Order of Saint John. This page contains the official legal authorizations required to publish the book in 18th-century France.</meta>

### APPROVAL.

I HAVE read, by order of My Lord the Keeper of the Seals <note>The *Garde des Sceaux* was the French Minister of Justice, responsible for overseeing the censorship and licensing of books.</note>, a Manuscript entitled, *History of the abolition of the Order of the Templars*; and I have found nothing therein that appeared to me ought to prevent its printing. At Paris, this 4th of November 1776.

->Signed, DE SANCY.<-

---

### ROYAL PRIVILEGE.

LOUIS <note>King Louis XVI, who reigned from 1774 to 1792.</note>, by the grace of God, King of France and of Navarre: To our beloved and faithful Councilors, the Members of our Courts of Parliament, Masters of Requests in ordinary of our Household, Grand Council, Provost of Paris, Bailiffs, Seneschals, their Civil Lieutenants, and others of our Justices, to whom it shall concern; GREETINGS. Our beloved Sieur <note>A formal title for a man of respectable standing.</note> BELIN, Bookseller in Paris, has represented to Us that he desires to have printed and given to the Public the *History of the abolition of the Order of the Templars*; should it please Us to grant him our necessary Letters of Permission. FOR THESE REASONS, wishing to treat the Petitioner favorably, We have permitted and do permit him by these Presents <note>A legal term referring to the document currently being read.</note>, to have said Work printed as many times as he shall see fit, and to have it sold and distributed throughout our Kingdom, during the term of five consecutive years, counting from the day of the date of these Presents. WE FORBID all Printers, Booksellers, and other persons, of whatever quality and condition they may be, from introducing any foreign printing of it into any—
